When
n>2(24)^{2}/\delta^{2}
, the last expression is smaller than
e^{-1}
.
And in this case the overall success of
B
is at least
(\gamma+\frac{\delta}{6})^{n}e^{-1}\geq\gamma^{n}\cdot(1+\frac{\delta}{6})^{n}e^{-1}\geq\gamma^{n}e^{\frac{\delta}{12}n-1}
, which is at least
\gamma^{n}
when
n\geq 12/\delta
.
∎

4
Proof of the Tensorization Lemma

See
5

Proof.

We start by proving that
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n})\leq\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)^{n}
.
Let
A
be a randomized algorithm with discounted score
\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)
.
Consider the algorithm
B
that computes
f^{n}
by running
n
independent instances of
A
sequentially on each of the
n
inputs.
By the independence of the
n
inputs and of the
n
instances of
A
, the discounted score of
B
over
\mu^{n}
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{ds}_{f^{n},\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(B)

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\sim\mu\\
D^{(1)},\ldots,D^{(n)}\sim A\end{subarray}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(D^{(i)}(x_{i}))\cdot e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(D^{(i)}(x_{i}))}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}\sim\mu\\
D^{(i)}\sim A\end{subarray}}\left[\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(D^{(i)}(x_{i}))\cdot e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(D^{(i)}(x_{i}))}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)^{n}.

Therefore,
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n})\leq\mathrm{ds}_{f^{n},\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(B)=\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)^{n}=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)^{n}
, as we wanted to show.

We now prove that
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n})\geq\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)^{n}
.
Let
B
be an algorithm that computes
f^{n}
and has discounted score
\mathrm{ds}_{f^{n},\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(B)=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n})
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
B
is deterministic.
We define a randomized algorithm
A
for
f
in the following way.

The algorithm
A
is defined with respect to a specific distribution
\nu
on the domain of
f^{n}
that depends on
f
,
\mu
,
D
, and
\alpha
in a way that will be specified later.
The behaviour of
A
is defined as follows.
First, it picks
i\in[n]
uniformly at random.
Next, it runs the algorithm
A_{i}
which works by running
B
on
n-1
fake inputs and the real input to
f
, with the real input placed in position
i
.
When
B
makes a query inside the
i
th input to
f
, the algorithm
A_{i}
queries the real input.
And when
B
makes a query to the
j
th input for some
j\neq i
, the answer to the query is generated artificially by the algorithm
A_{i}
; it generates the query answer from the conditional distribution
\nu
conditioned on the transcript of
B
so far.
(That is, it samples a string
y
from
\nu
conditioned on the string being consistent with all the queries answered so far, and it determines the value of the query by making the corresponding query to
y
.)

Let
\pi_{i}
be the distribution over the leaves of
D
reached by this simulation by
A_{i}
, assuming the real input (the one at position
i
) was sampled from
\mu
.
Extend
\pi_{i}
to a distribution over the domain of
f^{n}
by replacing each leaf of
B
with a sample from
\mu^{n}
conditioned on reaching that leaf.
The behaviour of
A_{i}
on an input sampled from
\mu
is then the same as the behaviour of
B
on an input sampled from
\pi_{i}
.
For an algorithm acting on
n
inputs to
f
, let
\mathrm{cost}_{i}
denote the number of queries to the
i
th input.
We now write

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)=\E_{i\in[n]}\left[\sum_{\ell\in B}\pi_{i}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell_{i})\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}_{i}(\ell)}\right]=\sum_{\ell\in B}\E_{i\sim[n]}\left[\pi_{i}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell_{i})\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}_{i}(\ell)}\right].

The positivity of the score measures guarantees that each term of the sum is non-negative.
By applying the AM-GM inequality to each term of the sum, the last expression is bounded below by

\sum_{\ell\in B}\E_{i\sim[n]}\left[\pi_{i}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell_{i})\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}_{i}(\ell)}\right]\geq\sum_{\ell}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell_{i})\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}_{i}(\ell)}\right)^{1/n}.

We next observe that
\sum_{i}\mathrm{cost}_{i}(\ell)=\mathrm{cost}(\ell)
.
By definition, we also have
\prod_{i}\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell_{i})=\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(\ell)
.
And then the key observation at the heart of the proof is that we also have

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}(\ell)=\nu(\ell)^{n-1}\mu^{n}(\ell).

To see why this last identity holds, note that we can write
\pi_{i}(\ell)=\prod_{(v,w)}\pi_{i}(w\mid v)
where the product is over all the directed edges
(v,w)
from the root of the tree to the leaf
\ell
. By our construction,

\pi_{i}(w|v)=\begin{cases}\mu^{n}(w|v)&\mbox{if node $v$ queries the $i$th instance}\\
\nu(w|v)&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

So we have

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{i}(\ell)=\prod_{(v,w)}\prod_{i}\pi_{i}(w|v)

\displaystyle=\prod_{(v,w)}\mu^{n}(w|v)\nu(w|v)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{(v,w)}\mu^{n}(w|v)\left(\prod_{(v,w)}\nu(w|v)\right)^{n-1}=\mu^{n}(\ell)\nu(\ell)^{n-1}.

Combining all of the above, we then end up with

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)\geq\sum_{\ell}\left(\nu(\ell)^{n-1}\mu^{n}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(\ell)\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}\right)^{1/n}.

Define
\nu
by setting

\nu(y)=\frac{\mu^{n}(y)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(B(y))\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(B(y))}}{\sum_{z}\mu^{n}(z)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(B(z))\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(B(z))}}.

This gives the corresponding distribution on leaves defined by

\nu(\ell)=\frac{\mu^{n}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(\ell)\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}}{\sum_{\ell^{\prime}}\mu^{n}(\ell^{\prime})\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(\ell^{\prime})\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell^{\prime})}},

and so we obtain

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(A)\geq\left(\sum_{\ell}\mu^{n}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu^{n}}(\ell)\,e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}\right)^{1/n}=\mathrm{ds}_{f^{n},\alpha}^{\mu^{n}}(B)^{1/n}.\qed

5
Proof of the Equivalence Lemma

5.1
Proof Overview

Both the upper and lower bounds in
Lemma

6
rely on an alternative characterization of discounted score and score-weighted cost in terms of a reweighted distribution on leaves.
Let us first introduce this distribution.

For any randomized algorithm
R
, let us denote the distribution
\pi_{R}
on the leaves of the deterministic decision trees in the support of
R
obtained by taking the probability that we reach this leaf when
D\sim R
and
x\sim\mu
and rescaling it by the weight
\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)
.
Namely, letting
\mu(x)
and
R(D)
denote the probability that
x\sim\mu
and
D\sim R
, respectively, we define the rescaled probability of the leaf
\ell
of
D
to be

\pi_{R}(\ell)=\frac{\mu(\ell)R(D)\cdot\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)}{\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)}.

The discounted score of the randomized algorithm
R
satisfies

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(R)=\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]

and the success-conditioned cost of an algorithm satisfies

\overline{\mathrm{scost}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)=\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)].

This means that we can express the maximum discounted score of
f
as

\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)=\max_{R}\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]

and its distributional success-conditioned query complexity of
f
can be expressed as

\hbox to12.14pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.06804pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.65277pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -8.1 6.2 L 8.1 6.2 M -8.1 7.98 L 8.1 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma}^{\mu}(f)=\min_{R\,:\,\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\geq\gamma}\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)].

With this characterization,
the lower bound in
Lemma

6
is an immediate consequence of Jensen’s inequality.

The upper bound requires a few more ingredients.
The first is
Proposition

28
, showing that for every success parameter
\gamma
, there exists a discount parameter
\alpha^{*}
for which the maximum discounted score
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)
is achieved by a randomized algorithm
R
with average score
\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)
exactly equal to
\gamma
.
Choosing the parameter
\alpha^{*}
and corresponding algorithm
R^{*}
in the conclusion of the proposition, we obtain the identity

\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)=\gamma\cdot\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}].

What we would like to use at this point is a “reverse Jensen inequality” to obtain a bound of the form

\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\leq}}e^{-c\,\alpha^{*}\cdot\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]}

for some constant
c
.
No such inequality holds in general, but we do have the fact that when
y
is a random variable bounded by some interval
[0,d]
, then convexity of the
e^{-y}
function implies that
e^{-y}\leq(1-\frac{y}{d})e^{-0}+\frac{y}{d}e^{-d}
and so

\E[e^{-y}]\leq 1-\frac{1-e^{-d}}{d}\E[y]\leq e^{-\frac{1-e^{-d}}{d}\E[y]}.

Applying this inequality to the random variable
y=\alpha^{*}\mathrm{cost}(R^{*}(x))
then gives exactly the type of inequality we are aiming for if we have the guarantee that the cost of the leaves in
R^{*}
is bounded above by
O(1/\alpha^{*})
.

In general, however, we do not have any such guarantee on the maximum cost of the algorithm
R^{*}
.
We can get around this limitation by considering a truncation of
R^{*}
that yields a different algorithm
R^{\dagger}
.
The key to completing the argument with this approach is to show that such a truncation does not affect the average cost by more than a constant multiplicative factor.
And this is where the fact that
R^{*}
has maximum discounted score for the parameter
\alpha^{*}
comes into play: we can argue that any such algorithm cannot have much of its average cost (even in the rescaled distribution
\pi_{R^{*}}
) on high-cost leaves.
The proof of this last fact is obtained by a contradiction argument, showing that if the last condition holds, we can define an algorithm with strictly better discounted score by truncating part of the tree of
R^{*}
, contradicting the fact that it has maximal discounted score.

5.2
The Proof

See
6

Proof.

By the characterization of maximum discounted score introduced in the proof overview,

\displaystyle\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle=\max_{R}\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]

\displaystyle\geq\max_{R\,:\,\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\geq\gamma}\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\cdot\E_{\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]

\displaystyle\geq\gamma\cdot\max_{R\,:\,\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\geq\gamma}\E_{\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}].

By Jensen’s inequality and the convexity of the function
y\mapsto e^{-\alpha y}
,
for every algorithm
R
we have the inequality
\E_{\pi_{R}}[e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]\geq e^{-\alpha\,\E_{\pi_{R}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma\cdot\max_{R\,:\,\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\geq\gamma}e^{-\alpha\,\E_{\pi_{R}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]}

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot e^{-\alpha\cdot\min_{R\,:\,\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)\geq\gamma}\E_{\pi_{R}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]}

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot e^{-\alpha\,\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to6.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83751pt\lower-2.39166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.48682pt}{-2.39166pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -6.49 4.42 L 6.49 4.42 M -6.49 5.67 L 6.49 5.67}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma}^{\mu}(f)}.

This completes the proof of the lower bound in the lemma.

We now complete the proof of the upper bound.
Let
\alpha^{*}
be the discount rate and
R^{*}
be the associated algorithm in the conclusion of
Proposition

28
.
These objects satisfy

\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)=\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R^{*})\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}]=\gamma\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}].

Let
d
be a depth parameter whose value will be set later.
The trivial inequality
\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}\leq\mathrm{cost}(\ell)
implies that
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)
can be bounded above by

\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)\leq\gamma\cdot\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}}].

By convexity of the function
e^{-\alpha^{*}\,y}
, when
y
is bounded by
0\leq y\leq d
, then

e^{-\alpha^{*}\,y}\leq\left(1-\frac{y}{d}\right)\cdot e^{-\alpha^{*}\cdot 0}+\frac{y}{d}\cdot e^{-\alpha^{*}\,d}=1-\frac{1-e^{-\alpha^{*}\,d}}{d}\cdot y.

Applying this inequality to the random variable
y=\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}
, we obtain

\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}}]\leq 1-\frac{1-e^{-\alpha^{*}\,d}}{d}\cdot\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}]\leq e^{-\frac{1-e^{-\alpha^{*}\,d}}{d}\cdot\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}]}.

Setting
d=2\ln(2)/\alpha^{*}
, we observe that

\frac{1-e^{-\alpha^{*}\,d}}{d}=\frac{1-2^{-2}}{2\ln(2)/\alpha^{*}}=\frac{3}{8\ln(2)}\cdot\alpha^{*}.

Combining all of the above gives us the inequality

\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)\leq\gamma\cdot e^{-c^{\prime}\alpha^{*}\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\lfloor D\rfloor_{d}(x))]}

for the constant
c^{\prime}=3/8\ln(2)
.
To complete the proof of the upper bound, it remains to bound
\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}]
from below by a multiplicative constant times
\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]
.

Claim 16
.

With
R^{*}
and
d
as defined above,
\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}]\geq\frac{1}{3}\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]
.

For any depth
\tau
, let us write
X_{>\tau}
to denote the event that
\mathrm{cost}(\ell)>\tau
and, in a slight abuse of notation, let us also write
X_{>\tau}
to denote the indicator random variable corresponding to this event.
Similarly, let
X_{\leq\tau}
denote the event that
\mathrm{cost}(\ell)\leq\tau
and its indicator random variable.

Let us write
L(\mu,R^{*})
to denote the distribution on the leaf
D(x)
reached when we draw
x\sim\mu
and
D\sim R^{*}
.
We can write the discounted score of
R^{*}
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(R^{*})

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)\cdot e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{\leq\tau}]+\E_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)\cdot e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{>\tau}]

\displaystyle=\E_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)\cdot e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{\leq\tau}]+\gamma\,\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{>\tau}].

Let
\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{\tau}
be the randomized algorithm by truncating
R^{*}
at depth
\tau
.
The truncation is completed by replacing each internal node at depth
\tau
in a tree
D
in the support of
R^{*}
with a leaf to obtain the tree
\lfloor D\rfloor_{\tau}
.
For every leaf
\ell
in a decision tree
D
in the support of
R^{*}
, let us write
\lfloor\ell\rfloor_{\tau}
to denote the leaf in
\lfloor D\rfloor_{\tau}
reached by the inputs that lead to
\ell
in
D
.
The cost of these leaves satisfies
\mathrm{cost}(\lfloor\ell\rfloor_{\tau})=\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),\tau\}
.
And the discounted score of the algorithm
\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{\tau}
is bounded below by

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{\tau})\geq\E_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{\leq\tau}]+\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\tau}\Pr_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[X_{>\tau}]

where the second term uses the bounded range property of scoring measures which guarantees that the score of every leaf (including in particular the ones introduced during the truncation process) is at least
\frac{1}{2}
.
Since
R^{*}
has maximum discounted score, we must have
\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(R^{*})\geq\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{\tau})
.
And so the above decompositions of the discounted scores of
R^{*}
and
\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{\tau}
imply that

\gamma\,\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{>\tau}]\geq\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\tau}\Pr_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[X_{>\tau}].

The right-hand side of this inequality can be further bounded below by noting that

\Pr_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[X_{>\tau}]\geq\E_{\ell\sim L(\mu,R^{*})}[\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)X_{>\tau}]=\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R^{*})\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}]=\gamma\,\Pr_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}].

And the left-hand side can be bounded above by using

\E_{\ell\sim\pi_{R^{*}}}[e^{-\alpha^{*}\,\mathrm{cost}(\ell)}X_{>\tau}]\leq\left(\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}]-\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau+d}]\right)e^{-\alpha^{*}\tau}+\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau+d}]e^{-\alpha^{*}(\tau+d)}.

Combining the last three inequalities, we obtain

\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau+d}](1-e^{-\alpha^{*}d})\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}].

Recalling that
d=2\ln(2)/\alpha^{*}
, this gives
\frac{3}{4}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau+d}]\leq\frac{1}{2}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}]
or, equivalently,

\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau+d}]\leq\frac{2}{3}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>\tau}].

We now use this inequality to complete the proof of the claim.

The expected cost of
R^{*}
under the distribution
\pi_{R^{*}}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]=\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{\leq d}]+\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{>d}].

The latter term can be bounded above by

\displaystyle\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{>d}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}(k+1)d\left(\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>kd}]-\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>(k+1)d}\right)

\displaystyle=d\sum_{k\geq 1}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>kd}].

From the inequality in the last paragraph, for every
k\geq 2
,

\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>kd}]\leq\tfrac{2}{3}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>(k-1)d}]\leq\left(\tfrac{2}{3}\right)^{k-1}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>d}].

So
\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{>d}]\leq d\sum_{k\geq 0}(\frac{2}{3})^{k}\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>d}]
and

\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]\leq\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{\leq d}]+3d\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>d}].

Meanwhile, the expected cost of
\lfloor R^{*}\rfloor_{d}
under
\pi_{R^{*}}
can be expressed as

\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\lfloor\ell\rfloor_{d})]=\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)X_{\leq d}]+d\Pr_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[X_{>d}].

Therefore,
\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]\leq 3\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\mathrm{cost}(\lfloor\ell\rfloor_{d})]=3\E_{\pi_{R^{*}}}[\min\{\mathrm{cost}(\ell),d\}]
, as we wanted to show.
∎

6
List-Decoding Direct Product Theorems

6.1
Preliminary Lemmas

Before we can complete the proof of
Theorem

2
, we need to establish a few information-theoretic facts.
The
entropy
of the random variable
X\sim\pi
over a finite domain is

H(X)=\sum_{x}\pi(x)\log\tfrac{1}{\pi(x)}=\E_{X\sim\pi}[\log\tfrac{1}{\pi(X)}].

The
cross-entropy
of
X\sim\pi
with respect to
Z\sim\sigma
over the same domain is

H(Z,X)=\E_{X\sim\sigma}[\log\tfrac{1}{\pi(X)}].

We start with a basic inequality on the entropy of collections of independent Bernoulli random variables.

Lemma 17
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
be
2n
independent Bernoulli random variables with
X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\frac{1+\delta_{i}}{2})
and
Z_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(\frac{1+\tau_{i}}{2})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
If
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
and
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
satisfy

H(Z)\geq n-\alpha

and

H(Z,X)\leq n-\beta

for some
\beta\leq\alpha
, then

H(X)\leq n-\frac{\beta^{2}}{16\alpha}.

Proof.

If there is any index
i
for which
\delta_{i}>\tau_{i}
, then the random variable
X^{\prime}
drawn as
X
except with bias
\tau_{i}
for
X_{i}
also satisfies the two constraints since
H(Z_{i},X_{i})\geq H(Z_{i})=H(Z_{i},X^{\prime}_{i})
and it has strictly larger entropy
H(X^{\prime})>H(X)
.
Therefore, without loss of generality, we can consider only the case where
\delta_{i}\leq\tau_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
.

The entropy of
X_{i}
satisfies

1-H(X_{i})=1-h\left(\frac{1+\delta_{i}}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2\ln 2}\left(\delta_{i}^{2}+\frac{\delta_{i}^{4}}{6}+\frac{\delta_{i}^{6}}{15}+\cdots\right).

So
\frac{\delta_{i}^{2}}{2\ln 2}\leq 1-H(X_{i})\leq\delta_{i}^{2}

and by adding the entropy of each coordinate of
X
,

\frac{\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}}{2\ln 2}\leq n-H(X)\leq\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}.

Similarly,
n-H(Z)\geq\|\tau\|_{2}^{2}/2\ln 2
.
With the condition in the lemma statement, we therefore have the bound

\|\tau\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\ln 2\cdot\alpha.

We also have

1-H(Z_{i},X_{i})=1-H(X_{i})+\frac{\tau_{i}-\delta_{i}}{2}\log(\frac{1+\delta_{i}}{1-\delta_{i}}).

Since
\log(1+x)\leq x/\ln(2)
, the last term in this expression is bounded by

\frac{\tau_{i}-\delta_{i}}{2}\log(\frac{1+\delta_{i}}{1-\delta_{i}})=\frac{\tau_{i}-\delta_{i}}{2}\log(1+\frac{2\delta_{i}}{1-\delta_{i}})\leq\frac{\tau_{i}-\delta_{i}}{2}\cdot\frac{2\delta_{i}}{\ln(2)(1-\delta_{i})}=\frac{\tau_{i}-\delta_{i}}{1-\delta_{i}}\cdot\frac{\delta_{i}}{\ln 2}\leq\frac{\tau_{i}\delta_{i}}{\ln 2},

and we obtain

n-H(Z,X)\leq n-H(X)+\frac{\left<\tau,\delta\right>}{\ln 2}\leq\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\|\tau\|_{2}\|\delta\|_{2}}{\ln 2}\leq\frac{2}{\ln 2}\,\|\tau\|_{2}\|\delta\|_{2}

where the last inequality uses the fact that
\|\delta\|_{2}\leq\|\tau\|_{2}\leq\frac{\|\tau\|_{2}}{\ln 2}
.

The above inequalities and the conditions of the lemma now give

\beta^{2}\leq\big(n-H(Z,X)\big)^{2}\leq\frac{4\|\tau\|_{2}^{2}\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}}{\ln^{2}2}\leq\frac{8}{\ln 2}\,\alpha\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}.

These inequalities imply that
\frac{\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}}{\ln 2}\geq\frac{\beta^{2}}{8\,\alpha}
.
Therefore,

n-H(X)\geq\frac{\|\delta\|_{2}^{2}}{2\ln 2}\geq\frac{\beta^{2}}{16\alpha}.\qed

We use the above bound to obtain the following result.

Lemma 18
.

Fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
\epsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]
.
If
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are biases and
L\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
is a list of
2^{n-\ell}
elements such
that when
X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p_{i})
are drawn independently,
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
satisfies

\Pr[X\in L]\geq 2^{-\epsilon\ell},

then
H(X)\leq n-\frac{(1-\epsilon)^{2}\ell}{320}
.

Proof.

Fix
\alpha=2^{-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}
and
d=\alpha 2^{(1-\epsilon)\ell-n}
.
Consider the disjoint subsets
B_{0},B_{1},B_{2},\ldots
of
L
where for each
i\geq 0
,

B_{i}=\{x\in L:2^{i}d<p(x)\leq 2^{i+1}d\}.

By the hypothesis of the lemma,
\sum_{x\in L}p(x)\geq 2^{-\epsilon\ell}
.
And by construction, the only elements in
L
that are not in one of the bins
B_{i}
,
i\geq 0
are the ones for
which
p(x)\leq d=\alpha 2^{(1-\epsilon)\ell-n}
, so the total mass of the excluded elements is at most
2^{n-\ell}\cdot\alpha 2^{(1-\epsilon)\ell-n}=\alpha 2^{-\epsilon\ell}
.
This means that the mass of the points in the union of the
B_{i}
sets is bounded below by

\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{x\in B_{i}}p(x)\geq(1-\alpha)2^{-\epsilon\ell}.

This lower bound implies that there must be an index
i\geq 0
for which the bin
B_{i}
has total mass

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:large-bin}\sum_{x\in B_{i}}p(x)\geq(1-\alpha)2^{-\epsilon\ell}\cdot 2^{-(i+1)}

(8)

otherwise we would have
\sum_{i\geq 0}\sum_{x\in B_{i}}p(x)<(1-\alpha)2^{-\epsilon\ell}\cdot 2^{-(i+1)}=(1-\alpha)2^{-\epsilon\ell}
, contradicting the lower bound on the mass of points in the union of the bins.
Fix an index
i
that satisfies
Eq.

8
and let
D
be the uniform distribution on the bin
B_{i}
.

The upper bound on the mass of each element in
B_{i}
and the lower bound on the total mass of this bin imply that the number of points in
B_{i}
is bounded below by

|B_{i}|\geq\frac{(1-\alpha)2^{-\epsilon\ell}\cdot 2^{-(i+1)}}{2^{i+1}d}=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\cdot 2^{n-\ell}\cdot 2^{-2(i+1)}.

So the entropy of
D
is bounded below by

H(D)=\log|B_{i}|\geq n-\big(\ell+2(i+1)-\log\tfrac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\big).

When
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{4}
, then
\log\tfrac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}=\log(2^{-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}}-1)\geq-2
, so in this case the lower bound simplifies to
H(D)\geq n-\big(\ell+4+2i\big)
.
Meanwhile, the lower bound on the mass of the elements in
B_{i}
implies that

H(D,X)=-\mathrm{E}_{x\sim D}[\log p(x)]\leq-\log(2^{i}d)=-\log(\alpha 2^{(1-\epsilon)\ell-n}2^{i})=n-\big((1-\epsilon)\ell-\log\tfrac{1}{\alpha}+i\big).

Since
\log\frac{1}{\alpha}=\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}
, we can simplify this bound to
H(D,X)\leq n-\big((1-\epsilon)\ell-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}+i\big)
.

Consider now the Bernoulli random variables
Z_{i}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(q_{i})
with parameters
q_{i}=\Pr_{x\in D}[x_{i}=1]
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Each
Z_{i}
is equal to the marginal
D_{i}
of
D
on the
i
th coordinate.
The random variable
Z
satisfies

H(Z)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(Z_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(D_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}H(D_{i}\mid D_{<i})=H(D)

and, since
p
is a product distribution,

H(Z,X)=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{E}_{x_{i}\sim Z_{i}}[\log p_{i}(x_{i})]=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{E}_{x_{i}\sim D_{i}}[\log p_{i}(x_{i})]=H(D,X).

Applying
Lemma

17
to
X
and
Z
, we obtain

H(X)\leq n-\frac{((1-\epsilon)\ell-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}+i)^{2}}{16(\ell+4+2i)}.

Since the ratio
\frac{((1-\epsilon)\ell-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2}+i)^{2}}{16(\ell+4+2i)}
is monotone in
i
when
\ell\geq 1
and
i\geq 0
, the entropy of
X
is bounded by

H(X)\leq n-\frac{((1-\epsilon)\ell-\frac{1-\epsilon}{2})^{2}}{16(\ell+4)}=n-\frac{(1-\epsilon)^{2}}{16}\cdot\frac{(\ell-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}{\ell+4}.

Note that when
\ell\geq 1
, then
\frac{(\ell-\frac{1}{2})^{2}}{\ell+4}\geq\frac{\ell}{20}
. Therefore,

H(X)\leq n-\frac{(1-\epsilon)^{2}\ell}{320}.\qed

6.2
Proof of the List-Decoding Direct Product Theorem

The proof of
Theorem

2
relies on one more technical observation.

Proposition 19
.

For any constant
\lambda>0
, if
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

\frac{2^{-\frac{1}{2k}}-2^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\geq\lambda

and
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2k}]
, then for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\frac{2^{-\epsilon\ell}-2^{-k\epsilon\ell}}{1-2^{-k\epsilon\ell}}\geq\lambda^{\ell}.

Proof.

As a first step,
define the function

g\colon y\mapsto(k-1)-ky+y^{k}.

This function satisfies
g(1)=0
.
Its derivative is
g^{\prime}(y)=-k+ky^{k-1}
, which is negative on all
y\in[0,1)
and positive on all
y>1
.
So the function
g
is non-negative in the entire range
[0,\infty)
.

Consider now the function
h\colon\epsilon\mapsto\frac{2^{-\epsilon}-2^{-k\epsilon}}{1-2^{-k\epsilon}}
.
The derivative of this function is

h^{\prime}(\epsilon)=\frac{2^{-(k+1)\epsilon}\ln 2}{(1-2^{-k\epsilon})^{2}}\cdot\big(-(k-1)+k2^{\epsilon}-(2^{\epsilon})^{k}\big)=-C_{k,\epsilon}\cdot g(2^{\epsilon})

with
C_{k,\epsilon}=\frac{2^{-(k+1)\epsilon}\ln 2}{(1-2^{-k\epsilon})^{2}}>0
for all
\epsilon>0
.
By the non-negativity of
g
, we then have that
h
is non-increasing on
[0,1]
and so every
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2k}]
satisfies

\frac{2^{-\epsilon}-2^{-k\epsilon}}{1-2^{-k\epsilon}}\geq\frac{2^{-\frac{1}{2k}}-2^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{1-2^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\geq\lambda.

To complete the proof, we now want to show that for every
a\in(0,1)
and every
\ell\geq 1
,

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:ratio-lb}\frac{a^{\ell}-a^{k\ell}}{1-a^{k\ell}}\geq\left(\frac{a-a^{k}}{1-a^{k}}\right)^{\ell}.

(9)

Establishing this inequality completes the proof of the proposition by taking
a=2^{-\epsilon}
and using the lower bound
\frac{2^{-\epsilon}-2^{-k\epsilon}}{1-2^{-k\epsilon}}\geq\lambda
established above.

Fix any
a\in(0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consider the function

\phi\colon\ell\mapsto\ln\left(\frac{a^{\ell}-a^{k\ell}}{1-a^{k\ell}}\right)-\ell\ln\left(\frac{a-a^{k}}{1-a^{k}}\right).

To complete the proof of
Eq.

9
, we want to show that
\phi(\ell)\geq 0
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
We already have
\phi(1)=0
.
So we want to show that
\phi^{\prime}(\ell)\geq 0
for all
\ell\geq 1
.
The derivative of
\phi
is

\phi^{\prime}(\ell)=\ln(a)\left(\frac{a^{\ell}-ka^{k\ell}}{a^{\ell}-a^{k\ell}}+\frac{ka^{\ell}}{1-a^{k\ell}}\right)-\ln\left(\frac{a-a^{k}}{1-a^{k}}\right).

Using the bound
\frac{a-a^{k}}{1-a^{k}}<a
that holds for all
a\in(0,1)
and
k>1
, we have that

\phi^{\prime}(\ell)\geq\ln(a)\left(\frac{a^{\ell}-ka^{k\ell}}{a^{\ell}-a^{k\ell}}+\frac{ka^{\ell}}{1-a^{k\ell}}-1\right).

Since
\ln(a)<0
, we obtain our proof that
\phi^{\prime}(\ell)\geq 0
if we can show that

\frac{a^{\ell}-ka^{k\ell}}{a^{\ell}-a^{k\ell}}+\frac{ka^{\ell}}{1-a^{k\ell}}\leq 1.

Expanding, re-arranging the terms, and simplifying, this inequality is equivalent to showing that

0\leq a^{k}\big((k-1)-ka+a^{k}\big)=a^{k}\cdot g(a).

As we have already seen,
g
is non-negative and so we also have that
\phi^{\prime}(\ell)\geq 0
, and as a result
Eq.

9
holds for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, completing the proof of the proposition.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof of
Theorem

2
.

See
2

Proof.

Let us first introduce and fix a few technical parameters.
Set
\lambda=2^{-\frac{1}{2560}}
.
Choose
k
to be the least positive integer that satisfies the inequality
\frac{2^{-1/2k}-2^{-1/2}}{1-2^{-1/2}}\geq\lambda
.
Let
\epsilon
be defined so that
\gamma=2^{-\epsilon}
.
Fix the universal constant
c
in the lemma statement to be
c=2^{-\frac{1}{2k}}
so that
\epsilon\leq\frac{1}{2k}
when
\gamma\geq c
.

Fix any distribution
\mu
on the domain of
f
.
Let
A
be a deterministic algorithm that computes
\mathrm{LD}_{\ell}^{n}\circ f
with a success probability of at least
2^{-\epsilon\ell}
on inputs drawn from
\mu^{n}
.
Let us define
\beta=2^{-k\epsilon\ell}
and
\alpha=\frac{2^{-\epsilon\ell}-\beta}{1-\beta}
.
The algorithm
A
has probability at least
\alpha
of landing in a leaf with success probability at least
\beta
since our choice of
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfies

\alpha\cdot 1+(1-\alpha)\beta=2^{-\epsilon\ell}.

By
Lemma

18
, the entropy loss score of each leaf with success probability at least
\beta=2^{-k\epsilon\ell}
is at least
2^{(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/320-n}
.
Therefore, the overall entropy loss score of
A
is at least

\alpha\cdot 2^{(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/320-n}
.
By the choice of
k
and
Proposition

19
, we have
\alpha\geq\lambda^{\ell}=2^{-\ell/2560}\geq 2^{-(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/640}
.
So the overall entropy score of
A
is at least
2^{(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/640-n}
.

The argument above implies that

\mathrm{R}_{2^{-\epsilon\ell}}(\mathrm{LD}_{\ell}^{n}\circ f)\geq\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{Ent}\,2^{(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/640-n}}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n}).

By the Direct Product Theorem with the entropy loss score, we also have the bound

\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{Ent}\,2^{(1-k\epsilon)^{2}\ell/640-n}}^{\mu^{n}}(f^{n})\geq\Omega\left(n\cdot\hbox to12.14pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.06804pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

Since the success of any leaf of a randomized algorithm for

is at least

,

6.3
Proof of the Threshold Direct Product Theorem

Proof of
Corollary

3
.

Let
A
be an algorithm that computes
\mathrm{Thr}_{k}^{n}\circ f
with success probability at least
\gamma^{\tau_{n,k}}
.
Let
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
denote the output of
A
on some input
x\in\{0,1\}^{m\times n}
.
Then the set

S_{y}=\{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[z_{i}\neq y_{i}]\leq n-k\}

includes the true value
z^{*}=(f(x^{(1)}),\ldots,f(x^{(n)})
if and only if
A
correctly computed
\mathrm{Thr}_{k}^{n}\circ f
on its execution.

Let
B
be the algorithm that simulates
A
and outputs the set
S_{y}
corresponding to
A
’s output
y
.
The size of the list that
B
outputs is
|S_{y}|=2^{n-\tau_{n,k}}
.
And it computes
\mathrm{LD}_{\tau_{n,k}}^{n}\circ f
correctly with success probabilty
\gamma^{\tau_{n,k}}
.
Therefore, by
Theorem

2
, the algorithm
A
simulated by
B
must have query complexity at least
\Omega(\tau_{n,k}\,\mathrm{R}(f))
.
∎

6.4
Proof of the Labelled Threshold DPT

Proof of
Corollary

4
.

Let
A
compute
\mathrm{Label}_{k}^{n}\circ f
with success probability
\gamma^{k}
. Consider the algorithm
B
that simulates
A
and that on
A
’s output
y\in\{0,1,*\}^{n}
returns the set

S_{y}=\big\{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\forall i\in[n],y_{i}\in\{z_{i},*\}\big\}.

This set has cardinality
|S_{y}|=2^{n-k}
and it includes the true value
(f(x^{(1)}),\ldots,f(x^{(n)}))
if and only if
y\in\mathrm{Label}_{k}^{n}\circ f
.
Therefore,
B
succeeds in computing
\mathrm{LD}_{k}^{n}\circ f
with probability at least
\gamma^{k}
and so by
Theorem

2
, the simulated algorithm
A
must have query complexity
\Omega(k\,\mathrm{R}(f))
.
∎

7
Score Amplification

7.1
Success Amplification by Boosting

Efficient success amplification is one of the key tools in the study of worst-case and average-case randomized complexity measures.
It is not a property shared by distributional complexity when we fix a single distribution.
(See
[
Sha03
,
BKST24
]
for example.)
But, interestingly and critically for the proof of the upper bound in
Theorem

1
, the maximum distributional complexity of functions
does
admit efficient success amplification.

Lemma 20
.

For every success probability
\gamma
in the range
\frac{1}{2}\leq\gamma\leq 1
and every Boolean-valued function
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
,

.

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\mu}_{3\gamma^{2}-2\gamma^{3}}\leq 4\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma}(f)
.
We will select three randomized algorithms
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
.
For each
i=1,2,3
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, let
\alpha_{i}(x)
denote the probability that
A_{i}
outputs the corrrect value
f(x)
.

Let
A_{1}
be a randomized algorithm with average success probability
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)\alpha_{1}(x)=\gamma
and average cost
\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A_{1})=\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\mu}_{\gamma}(f)
against
\mu
.

Define
\nu
to be the distribution on
\mathcal{X}
obtained by setting

\nu(x)=\mu(x)\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)\alpha_{1}(x)+\gamma(1-\alpha_{1}(x))}{2\gamma(1-\gamma)}\right)

for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
. We can verify that
\nu
is indeed a valid distribution since
\nu(x)\geq 0
for all
x
and

\sum_{x}\nu(x)=\frac{\sum_{x}\mu(x)\alpha_{1}(x)}{2\gamma}+\frac{\sum_{x}\mu(x)(1-\alpha_{1}(x))}{2(1-\gamma)}=1.

We select
A_{2}
to be a randomized algorithm with average success probability
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\nu(x)\alpha_{2}(x)=\gamma
and average cost
\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\nu}(A_{2})=\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\nu}_{\gamma}(f)
against
\nu
.

Define
\pi
to be the distribution on
\mathcal{X}
obtained by setting

\pi(x)=\mu(x)\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}(x)(1-\alpha_{2}(x))+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)}{\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)]}\right).

We select
A_{3}
to be a randomized algorithm with average success probability
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\pi(x)\alpha_{3}(x)=\gamma
and average cost
\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\pi}(A_{3})=\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}^{\pi}_{\gamma}(f)
against
\pi
.

We combine the algorithms
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, and
A_{3}
to obtain an algorithm
B
for
f
that proceeds as follows.
On input
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the algorithm
B
:

1.

Runs
A_{1}
on
x
, obtains the value
y_{1}
.

2.

Runs
A_{2}
on
x
, obtains the value
y_{2}
.

3.

If
y_{1}=y_{2}
,
B
outputs the same value.

4.

Otherwise,
B
runs
A_{3}
on
x
and outputs its value.

We now analyze the correctness and the cost of the algorithm
B
.

Correctness analysis.

We want to show that
B
has average success probability at least
3\gamma^{2}-2\gamma^{3}
when
x
is drawn from
\mu
.

The probability that
A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)
and that
A_{3}(x)=f(x)
is

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)\left(\alpha_{1}(x)(1-\alpha_{2}(x))+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)\right)\alpha_{3}(x)=\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)]\cdot\sum_{x}\pi(x)\alpha_{3}(x).

By the correctness guarantee on
A_{3}
,
\sum_{x}\pi(x)\alpha_{3}(x)=\gamma
.
And

\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)]=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)\left(\alpha_{1}(x)(1-\alpha_{2}(x))+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)\right).

The guarantee on
A_{1}
implies that
\sum_{x}\mu(x)\alpha_{1}(x)=\gamma
so that

\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)\wedge A_{3}(x)=f(x)]=\gamma^{2}+\gamma\sum_{x}\mu(x)\left((1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)-\alpha_{1}(x)\alpha_{2}(x)\right).

Also,

\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)=A_{2}(x)=f(x)]=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)\alpha_{1}(x)\alpha_{2}(x).

So the overall probability that
B
outputs the correct value
f(x)
is

\displaystyle\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[B(x)=f(x)]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)=A_{2}(x)=f(x)]+\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)\wedge A_{3}(x)=f(x)]

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}+\sum_{x}\mu(x)\left((1-\gamma)\alpha_{1}(x)+\gamma(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\right)\alpha_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}+2\gamma(1-\gamma)\sum_{x}\nu(x)\alpha_{2}(x)

and the correctness guarantee on
A_{2}
implies that
\sum_{x}\nu(x)\alpha_{2}(x)=\gamma
which means that the overall success probability is
\gamma^{2}+2\gamma^{2}(1-\gamma)=3\gamma^{2}-2\gamma^{3}
, as we wanted to show.

Cost analysis.

The average cost of
A_{1}
against
\mu
is bounded by

\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A_{1})=\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

Let
c_{2}(x)
denote the average cost of
A_{2}
on input
x
.
The average cost of
A_{2}
with respect to
\mu
is

\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A_{2})=\sum_{x}\mu(x)c_{2}(x)=\sum_{x}\frac{2}{\alpha_{1}(x)/\gamma+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))/(1-\gamma)}\nu(x)c_{2}(x).

For any input
x
,
0\leq\alpha_{1}(x)\leq 1
and so

\frac{2}{\alpha_{1}(x)/\gamma+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))/(1-\gamma)}\leq 2\max\{\gamma,1-\gamma\}\leq 2.

Also, the cost guarantee on
A_{2}
implies that
\sum_{x}\nu(x)c_{2}(x)\leq\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

\lxSVG@closescope {{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Finally, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the algorithm
B
runs
A_{3}
with probability
\alpha_{1}(x)(1-\alpha_{2}(x))+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)
.
So if we let

denote the expected cost of
A_{3}
on input
x
, the average contribution of
A_{3}
to the overall cost of
B
is

\sum_{x}\mu(x)\big(\alpha_{1}(x)(1-\alpha_{2}(x))+(1-\alpha_{1}(x))\alpha_{2}(x)\big)c_{3}(x)=\Pr_{x\sim\mu}[A_{1}(x)\neq A_{2}(x)]\sum_{x}\pi(x)c_{3}(x).

The cost guarantee on
A_{3}
implies that
\sum_{x}\pi(x)c_{3}(x)\leq\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

The boosting lemma immediately yields the score amplification result stated in the preliminaries, which we restate here for convenience.

See
8

Proof.

This lemma follows immediately from the last one by observing that the difference in the success probability
3\gamma^{2}-2\gamma^{3}
after boosting and the original success probability
\gamma
is

3\gamma^{2}-2\gamma^{3}-1=(2\gamma-1)(1-\gamma).

When
\frac{1+\delta}{2}\leq\gamma\leq\frac{2}{3}
, this difference is bounded below by
(2\gamma-1)(1-\gamma)\geq\big(2(\tfrac{1+\delta}{2})-1\big)\big(1-\tfrac{2}{3}\big)=\frac{\delta}{3}
.
And when
\frac{2}{3}\leq\gamma\leq 1-\delta
, we have
(2\gamma-1)(1-\gamma)\geq\big(2(\tfrac{2}{3})-1\big)\big(1-(1-\delta)\big)=\frac{\delta}{3}
.
∎

When we use Hellinger score, we can get more efficient success amplification where the multiplicative factor is linear instead of quadratic in
\frac{1}{\delta}
.

The technical lemma at the heart of the proof is stated more naturally in terms of
Hellinger loss
instead of Hellinger score.
Define the Hellinger loss of a leaf
\ell
with respect to
f
and
\mu
to be
{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)=2\mathrm{Hel}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)-1
.
The Hellinger loss of a randomized algorithm is the expected Hellinger loss of its leaves:

\overline{{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}}_{f}^{\mu}(R)=\E_{D\sim R}\E_{\ell\sim D(\mu)}{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}_{f}^{\mu}(\ell)
.
We write
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Proof.

Fix any distribution
\mu
on the inputs to
f
.
Take
A
to be a randomized decision tree with Hellinger loss at most
\alpha
and average cost at most
\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}\,\alpha}(f)
with respect to
\mu
.

For any set leaf
\ell
of a decision tree in the support of
A
, define the distribution

\nu_{\ell}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{\mu(x)}{2\mu(\ell_{0})}&\mbox{if }x\in\ell_{0}\\
\frac{\mu(x)}{2\mu(\ell_{1})}&\mbox{if }x\in\ell_{1}\\
0&\mbox{if }x\notin\ell.\end{cases}

Let
B_{\ell}
denote a randomized algorithm with Hellinger loss at most
\beta
and cost at most
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

against the distribution
\nu_{\ell}
.

Consider now the randomized algorithm
C
defined by the following procedure:
First, we run
A
on the input
x
.
Let
\ell
denote the leaf of the tree reached by input
x
and the subset of inputs
\{0,1\}^{m}
that lead to this leaf.
Then we run
B_{\ell}
on
x
.

The Hellinger loss of the algorithm
C
is bounded by

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}}_{f}^{\mu}(C)

\displaystyle=\E_{D\sim A}\sum_{\ell\in D}\E_{D^{\prime}\sim B_{\ell}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}}2\sqrt{\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{0})\mu(\ell^{\prime}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\E_{D\sim A}\sum_{\ell\in D}\E_{D^{\prime}\sim B_{\ell}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}}2\sqrt{\mu(\ell_{0})\mu(\ell_{1})}\cdot 2\sqrt{\nu_{\ell}(\ell^{\prime}_{0})\nu_{\ell}(\ell^{\prime}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\E_{D\sim A}\sum_{\ell\in D}2\sqrt{\mu(\ell_{0})\mu(\ell_{1})}\cdot\left(\E_{D^{\prime}\sim B_{\ell}}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}}2\sqrt{\nu_{\ell}(\ell^{\prime}_{0})\nu_{\ell}(\ell^{\prime}_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\cdot\beta.

By construction, the expected cost of
A
with respect to
\mu
is at most
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

.

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

Since Hellinger score
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
is equivalent to Hellinger loss

, the above identity implies that

\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{{\mathrm{Hel}_{\downarrow}}\,e^{-1}}(f)=O\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

The lemma then follows from observing that
\mathrm{R}(f)=O(\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

for any constant
c
bounded away from
0
and
1
.
∎

The linear amplification lemma for Hellinger score also immediately implies an analogous lemma for exponential entropy score.

Lemma 22
.

For any
\alpha
,
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
,

\mathrm{R}(f)=O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\mathrm{Ent}\,2^{\alpha-1}}(f)\right).

Proof.

For any
x\in[0,1]
, the convexity of the function
2^{-x}
implies that
2^{-x}\leq 1-\frac{x}{2}
.
As a result, the exponential entropy score of a leaf with density
q
is

q^{q}(1-q)^{q}=2^{-h(q)}\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}h(q)=\frac{1+(1-h(q))}{2}.

The function
1-h(q)
is in turn bounded above by

1-h(q)\leq\frac{1}{\ln 2}(1-2\sqrt{q(1-q)}).

Consider any randomized algorithm
R
with exponential entropy score at least
2^{\alpha-1}
with respect to
f
and a fixed distribution
\mu
.
Then with the two inequalities above,

2^{\alpha-1}\leq\overline{\mathrm{Ent}}^{\mu}_{f}(R)=\E_{\ell}2^{-h(\mu(\ell_{1})/\mu(\ell))}\leq\frac{1+(\ln 2)^{-1}\E_{\ell}\big[1-2\sqrt{\frac{\mu(\ell_{1})}{\mu(\ell)}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{0})}{\mu(\ell)}}\big]}{2}.

This inequality and the basic inequality
1+\ln(2)\alpha\leq 2^{\alpha}
imply

\ln(2)^{2}\alpha\leq\E_{\ell}\left[1-2\sqrt{\frac{\mu(\ell_{1})}{\mu(\ell)}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{0})}{\mu(\ell)}}\right].

And the Hellinger score of
R
is therefore bounded below by

\overline{\mathrm{Hel}}^{\mu}_{f}(R)=\E_{\ell}\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\mu(\ell_{1})}{\mu(\ell)}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{0})}{\mu(\ell)}}\right]=\frac{1+\E_{\ell}\left[1-2\sqrt{\frac{\mu(\ell_{1})}{\mu(\ell)}\cdot\frac{\mu(\ell_{0})}{\mu(\ell)}}\right]}{2}\geq\frac{1+\ln(2)^{2}\alpha}{2}.

We have just shown that for every distribution
\mu
, every randomized algorithm
R
with entropy score
\overline{\mathrm{Ent}}^{\mu}_{f}(R)\geq 2^{\alpha-1}
also has Hellinger score
\overline{\mathrm{Hel}}^{\mu}_{f}(R)\geq\frac{1+\ln(2)^{2}\alpha}{2}
. As a result,
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\mathrm{Ent}\,2^{\alpha-1}}(f)\leq\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\mathrm{Hel}\,\frac{1+\ln(2)^{2}\alpha}{2}}(f)
and the lemma is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

9
.
∎

8
Relations Between the Different Complexity Measures

We prove various relations between different complexity measures in this section.

8.1
Worst-Case and Average-Case Complexity

Relations between worst-case and average-case complexity measures are well known. We include them here for the convenience of the reader.

As a starting point, by definition, the worst-case and average-case randomized query complexity measures always satisfy the inequality

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(f)\leq\mathrm{R}_{\gamma}(f).

In the other direction, we have the following inequalities.

Proposition 23
.

For every
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
and every function
f
,

\mathrm{R}_{\gamma}(f)\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\min\{\gamma-\frac{1}{2},1-\gamma\}}\cdot\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(f)\right).

Proof.

Consider first the small-advantage regime where
\gamma=\frac{1+\delta}{2}
.
Let
A
be a randomized algorithm with success probability
\gamma
and average cost
\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(f)
.
Let
A^{\prime}
be the algorithm obtained by truncating
A
and forcing it to guess the value of
f
whenever
A
exceeds its expected cost by a multiplicative factor of
4/\delta
.
By Markov’s inequality, the probability that this truncation event occurs is at most
\delta/4
, so that the success probability of
A^{\prime}
is at least
\frac{1+\delta/2}{2}
and we obtain the desired bound on the worst-case complexity for success probability
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
by applying standard success amplification.

For the small-error regime where
\gamma=1-\epsilon
, we use the same argument with truncation factor
1/\epsilon
.
∎

Proposition

23
implies that the average-case and worst-case randomized query complexity measures are asymptotically equivalent in the bounded-error regime, when
\gamma
is bounded away from both
\frac{1}{2}
and
1
by a constant.
In the small-bias and small-error regimes, however, there are functions whose average-case randomized query complexity is asymptotically smaller than their worst-case randomized query complexity.
For the small-bias regime, the bound in the last proposition is optimal.

Proposition 24
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
such that for every
\delta=\delta(n)>0

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)\leq\delta\cdot\mathrm{R}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f).

Proof.

Consider the parity function
\oplus_{n}\colon x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\pmod{2}
.
For any
\delta>0
,
\mathrm{R}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(\oplus_{n})=n
.
But in the average-case complexity setting, the (lazy) algorithm that with probability
\delta
queries everything to compute the value of
\oplus_{n}(x)
and otherwise guesses the value of the function has average cost
\delta n=\delta\,\mathrm{R}(\oplus_{n})
and succeeds with probability
\delta+(1-\delta)\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1+\delta}{2}
on every input.
∎

Note that by contrast there are also other functions for which
\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)=\Theta(\mathrm{R}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f))
.
One example of such a function is the Majority function when
\delta=1/n
.

There can also be asymptotic separations between the average-case and worst-case randomized query complexities in the small-error regime.

Proposition 25
.

For infinitely many values of
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a partial function
f\colon X\to\{0,1\}
,
X\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
, such that for every
\epsilon=\epsilon(n)>0
,

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{1-\epsilon}(f)\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\log(1/\epsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{R}_{1-\epsilon}(f)\right).

Proof.

Fix any even value of
n
and define the set

X=\left\{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}:\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}x_{2i-1}=\tfrac{n}{2}\wedge\exists b\in\{0,1\}\forall i\in[\tfrac{n}{2}]\ x_{2i-1}=1\Rightarrow x_{2i}=b\right\}.

For each
x\in X
, we define
f(x)
to be the value of
b
for which the last condition is satisfied.

Informally, we can think of
f
as containing
\frac{n}{2}
bins. Half of the bins are useful in that the second bit of the bin determines the value of the function and the other half are useless. Furthermore, the first bit of each bit indicates whether the bin is useful or not.

An algorithm that computes
f
with error at most
\epsilon
must find a useful bin with probability at least
1-O(\epsilon)
.
This can be guaranteed only when the worst-case query complexity of the algorithm is
\Omega(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.
So
\mathrm{R}_{1-\epsilon}(f)=\Omega(\log\frac{1}{\epsilon})
.

But the average-case cost of the algorithm that examines bins uniformly at random follows a geometric distribution with success parameter
\frac{1}{2}
and so
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{1-\epsilon}(f)=O(1)
.
∎

There are also total functions that exhibit an analogous gap between the worst-case and average-case query complexity; see
[
BB19
]
.

8.2
Maximum Distributional Complexity

Any algorithm with score
\gamma
and average cost at most
c
on every input
x
also has the same score and cost guarantees over all distributions
\mu
on the inputs.
So for every
f
and
\gamma
,

\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma}(f)\leq\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(f).

This inequality is asymptotically optimal in the bounded-error regime.
It is also asymptotically optimal in the small-error regime, where Vereshchagin
[
Ver98
]
showed that

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{1-\epsilon}(f)\leq 2\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{

.

In the small-advantage regime, we have the following relation between the maximum distributional and average-case complexity.

Proposition 26
.

For every function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
and
\delta=\delta(n)>0
, the average-case and maximum distributional complexity measures for success probability
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
are related by

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)\leq O\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)\right).

Proof.

We adapt Vereshchagin’s argument
[
Ver98
]
for the result mentioned above to the small-advantage regime.

Fix any pair of distributions
\mu
and
\nu
.
For every
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
, let
\pi_{\lambda}=\lambda\mu+(1-\lambda)\nu
.
And let
A_{\lambda}
be a randomized algorithm with success at leat
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
and average cost at most
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
