{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)
when both are measured against
\mu
.
The success probability of
A_{\lambda}
satisfies

\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\pi_{\lambda}}(A_{\lambda})=\lambda\cdot\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\mu}(A_{\lambda})+(1-\lambda)\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\nu}(A_{\lambda}).

Since
\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\mu}(A_{\lambda})\leq 1
, the success of
A_{\lambda}
against
\nu
is bounded below by

\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\nu}(A_{\lambda})\geq\frac{(1+\delta)/2-\lambda}{1-\lambda}.

Similarly, the average cost of
A_{\lambda}
satisfies

\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\pi_{\lambda}}(A_{\lambda})=\lambda\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A_{\lambda})+(1-\lambda)\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\nu}(A_{\lambda})

so that

\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A_{\lambda})\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f).

By choosing
\lambda=\delta/4
, we obtain an algorithm
A
with success probability
\overline{\mathrm{success}}_{f}^{\nu}(A)\geq\frac{1+\delta/2}{2}
and average cost
\overline{\mathrm{cost}}^{\mu}(A)\leq\frac{4}{\delta}\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)
.
This means that

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta/2}{2}}(f)\leq\frac{4}{\delta}\cdot\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)

and the result now follows from success amplification for average-case complexity.
∎

The bound in
Proposition

26
is qualitatively tight in that there can be a polynomial factor of
\delta
that separates the average-case and maximum distributional complexity of functions.

Proposition 27
.

For infinitely many
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a partial function
f\colon X\to\{0,1\}
,
X\subseteq\{0,1\}^{n}
such that for all
\delta=\delta(n)>0
,

\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)=O\left(\delta^{\frac{1}{6}}\,\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)\right).

Proof.

Consider the following construction in which we split the
n
coordinates into
k
blocks
B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}
of
n/k
variables each.
We construct the function so that there are two types of blocks: hard blocks and easy blocks.
The first variable in each block determines the type of a block, so that the algorithm can tell if a block is hard or easy with a single query.
We also associate a value with each block.
For each easy block, the value is determined by the second variable in the block, so that the value of an easy block is determined with only one additional query.
The value of a hard block, by contrast, is the parity of
1/\delta
variables, so that this value can only be determined by making
1/\delta
queries to the block.

Formally, writing
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as
x=(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(k)})
with each
x^{(i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n/k}
, we define the function
g\colon\{0,1\}^{n/k}\to\{0,1\}
by

g(y)=\begin{cases}y_{2}&\mbox{if }y_{1}=0\\
y_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus y_{1/\delta+1}&\mbox{if }y_{1}=1.\end{cases}

Then the function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}
is

f(x)=\mathrm{Maj}(g(x^{(1)}),\ldots,g(x^{(k)})).

Let us consider the partial function obtained by restricting
f
’s domain
X
to one of two possible types of input:

Many hard blocks:

For inputs of this type, there are exactly
\sqrt{\delta}k
blocks that are hard. And their values are balanced: exactly half of them have value 0 and the other half have value 1. The values of the easy blocks have
\delta
bias towards the value of the function. (I.e., for inputs of this type, the function
f
is the Gap Majority function on the values of the easy blocks.)

Few hard blocks:

For inputs of this type, exactly
\delta k
blocks are hard. Each of these blocks has the same value, whereas the values of the easy blocks are balanced. As a result, the value of the function is the value of each of the hard blocks.

We now prove the two complexity bounds separately.
First, we establish the upper bound

\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)=O(1/\delta^{3})

on the maximum distributional complexity of
f
.
We do so by considering two natural algorithms for computing
f
.
Algorithm
A
picks a random block. If the block is easy, it outputs its value; otherwise it just guesses.
Algorithm
B
picks a random block. If the block is hard, it computes its value and outputs it; otherwise it just guesses.

Fix now any distribution
\mu
on the domain of the partial function.
Let
p
denote the probability that an input drawn from
\mu
has many hard blocks.

Consider first the case where
p\geq\delta^{\frac{1}{6}}
.
Algorithm
A
has success probability at least
\frac{1+p\delta}{2}
on inputs drawn from
\mu
. By extending the algorithm so that it queries the value of
O(1/p^{2})
easy blocks instead of a single one (ignoring hard blocks, as before), we obtain an algorithm
A^{\prime}
with success probability
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
and average cost
O(1/p^{2})\leq O(\delta^{-1/3})
with respect to
\mu
.

Consider now the case where
p<\delta^{\frac{1}{6}}
.
Algorithm
B
has success probability at least
\frac{1+(1-p)\delta}{2}
on inputs drawn from
\mu
. By considering the extension of this algorithm that queries two blocks to see if either of them is hard, and outputting the value of a hard block if one is found, we obtain algorithm
B^{\prime}
with success probability at least
\frac{1+(1-p)(2\delta-\delta^{2})}{2}
against
\mu
, which is at least
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
when
p\leq\delta^{\frac{1}{6}}
. And the average cost of
B^{\prime}
over
\mu
is

2+p(2\sqrt{\delta}-\delta)(1/\delta)+(1-p)(2\delta-\delta^{2})(1/\delta)\leq 2+2p/\sqrt{\delta}+2=O(p/\sqrt{\delta}),

which is
O(\delta^{-1/3})
when
p<\delta^{\frac{1}{6}}
.

Combining the two observations above, we obtain that for any distribution
\mu
on domain
X
of
f
, there is an algorithm (either
A^{\prime}
or
B^{\prime}
) with success probability
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
against
\mu
and average cost
O(\delta^{-1/3})
with respect to the same distribution.
Therefore, by definition
O(\delta^{-1/3})
is an upper bound on
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)
.

To complete the proof of the proposition, we now show that the average-case complexity of
f
is bounded below by

\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}(f)=\Omega(\delta^{-1/2}).

To do so, assume on the contrary that there is an algorithm that computes
f
with success probability at least
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
and average cost
q=o(\sqrt{1/\delta})
.
Any algorithm with this cost can only check
o(\sqrt{1/\delta})
blocks to see if they are hard or easy and it can compute the value of at most that number of easy blocks.
The goal of an algorithm seeking to minimize its average cost is to avoid computing the value of any hard block when there are many of them.

Without loss of generality, we can consider algorithms that decide whether to compute a hard block based on (i) the number of hard blocks observed; and (ii) the bias of the easy blocks that is computed.
Note that when there are many hard blocks, the easy blocks have bias
\delta
towards the function value, whereas when there are few hard blocks, the easy blocks’s values are balanced. However, with at most
o(1/\sqrt{\delta})
easy block values, an algorithm cannot distinguish between these two cases with bias more than
o(1)
. Therefore, without loss of generality we can consider algorithms that use only the number of hard blocks observed to determine whether they should compute the value of one of those blocks.

When we run an algorithm with query complexity
o(\sqrt{1/\delta})
on inputs that have few hard blocks,
the probability that the algorithm finds at least 1 block is at most
q\cdot\delta=o(\sqrt{\delta})
.
And the probability that it finds more than one hard block is at most
\binom{q}{2}\delta^{2}=o(\delta)
.
So to have success probability at least
\frac{1+\delta}{2}
, its probability of computing a hard block (over all possible biases of the easy blocks) when there is exactly one such block must be at least
\sqrt{\delta}
.
But then the probability that the algorithm sees exactly 1 hard block on inputs that have many hard blocks is constant. Which means that its average cost on the uniform distribution over those inputs is at least
\sqrt{\delta}\cdot 1/\delta=1/\delta^{2}
.
∎

We remark that the last proposition also implies a polynomial separation between average-case and maximum distributional complexity: there exists a partial function
f
and success parameter
\gamma
for which
\hbox to8.19pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.09583pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.37 6.2 L 5.37 6.2 M -5.37 7.98 L 5.37 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma}(f)=O\left(\overline{\mathrm{R}}_{\gamma}(f)^{2/3}\right)
.
We have not tried to determine if the same is true for total function (though we suspect that it is) and what is the largest polynomial separation that we could achieve between the two complexity measures.

8.3
Score-Weighted Complexity

8.3.1
Relation to Maximum Distributional Complexity

We can give an upper bound as well as a lower bound of the score-weighted query complexity of randomized algorithms in terms of their maximum distributional complexity.

See
10

Proof.

, we can express the score-weighted cost of

as

8.3.2
Relation to Worst-Case Query Complexity

We can also upper bound score-weighted complexity with worst-case complexity for every function and for their direct products.

See
11

Proof.

Fix any distribution
\mu
on the domain of
f^{n}
.
Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
denote the set of randomized algorithms with score at least
\gamma^{n}
on every input of
f^{n}
, maximum cost
\mathrm{R}_{\gamma^{n}}(f^{n})
.
The set
\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
is non-empty since by definition there is an algorithm that satisfies these two properties.
And every leaf
\ell
of the decision trees in the support of the randomized algorithms in this set have score
\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu}(\ell)\geq(\frac{1}{2})^{n}>0
by the boundedness of scoring measures.

For any
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\mu}
and leaf
\ell
of
R
,

\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{cost}(\ell)\leq\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}^{\mu}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{R}_{\gamma^{n}}(f^{n}).

So

\overline{\mathrm{scost}}^{\mu}(R)=\frac{\E_{\ell}[\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{cost}(\ell)]}{\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f^{n}}^{\mu}(R)}\leq\frac{\E_{\ell}[\mathrm{score}_{f^{n}}(\ell)\cdot\mathrm{R}_{\gamma^{n}}(f^{n})]}{\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f^{n}}^{\mu}(R)}=\mathrm{R}_{\gamma^{n}}(f^{n})

and
\hbox to12.14pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.06804pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.65277pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}{
{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -8.1 6.2 L 8.1 6.2 M -8.1 7.98 L 8.1 7.98}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\gamma^{n}}(f^{n})=\max_{\mu}\hbox to12.14pt{\vbox to9.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.06804pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

8.4
Score and Discounted Score

One of the basic properties of the discounted score measure that we will require in the proof of
Lemma

6
is that for any score parameter
\gamma
in the non-trivial range between the score of the guessing algorithm and
1
, there exists some discount rate
\alpha^{*}
for which the maximum discounted score
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)
is achieved by a randomized algorithm
R
with score exactly equal to
\gamma
.

Proposition 28
.

Fix any function
f
and distribution
\mu
.
For every success parameter
\gamma
that is at least as large as the success probability of the trivial guessing algorithm, there exists a discount rate
\alpha^{*}
and a randomized algorithm
R^{*}
that satisfies

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(R^{*})=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(R^{*})=\gamma.

Proof.

Note first that since the discounted score of a randomized decision tree
R
is by definition

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(R)=\E_{D\sim R}\E_{x\sim\mu}[\mathrm{score}_{f}^{\mu}(D(x))\cdot e^{-\alpha\,\mathrm{cost}(D(x))}],

there is always a deterministic decision tree
D
in the support of
R
with discounted score at least as large as that of
R
.
As a result, the maximum discounted score
\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha}^{\mu}(f)
is always attained by a deterministic decision tree.

For each deterministic decision tree
T
, define the function
\phi_{T}\colon\alpha\mapsto\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(T)
.
The function
\phi_{T}
is continuous.
Then for any two trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, the function
\phi_{T_{1}}-\phi_{T_{2}}
is also continuous.
As a result, the set
\{\alpha:\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(T_{1})\geq\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(T_{2})\}
, which is the preimage of
\phi_{T_{1}}-\phi_{T_{2}}
on the closed set
[0,\infty)
, is a closed set.

Let us write
S_{\geq},S_{\leq}\subseteq[0,\infty)
to represent the sets of discount rates for which the maximum discount score is achieved by a deterministic algorithm with score at least and at most
\gamma
, respectively.
The set
S_{\geq}
is closed, since we can write it as the finite union of finite intersections of closed sets

S_{\geq}=\bigcup_{T_{1}:\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(T_{1})\geq\gamma}\bigcap_{T_{2}}\{\alpha:\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(T_{1})\geq\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha}^{\mu}(T_{2})\}.

Similarly,
S_{\leq}
is also closed.
And these two closed sets partition their universe, i.e.,
S_{\geq}\cup S_{\leq}=[0,\infty)
.
As a result, their intersection
S_{\geq}\cap S_{\leq}
is not empty.
Let
\alpha^{*}
be any element in this intersection.

By construction, for our choice of
\alpha^{*}
there exist two deterministic algorithms
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
that satisfy

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(T_{1})=\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(T_{2})=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)

and

\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(T_{1})=\gamma-\delta_{1},\quad\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(T_{2})=\gamma+\delta_{2}

for some parameters
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\geq 0
.
Define the randomized algorithm
A
to be the convex combination

A=\frac{\delta_{2}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}T_{1}+\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}T_{2}.

The discounted score of
A
is

\mathrm{ds}_{f,\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(A)=\mathrm{DS}_{\alpha^{*}}^{\mu}(f)

and its score is

\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(A)=\frac{\delta_{2}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(T_{1})+\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}\overline{\mathrm{score}}_{f}^{\mu}(T_{2})=\frac{\delta_{2}(\gamma-\delta_{1})+\delta_{1}(\gamma+\delta_{2})}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}=\gamma.\qed
